Alternatives not chosen: a fourth section on the document page (already up to 500 rows per category); the job page (jobs are forgotten when the server stops, the audiobook is not). Reason: a page per document that outlasts the server run, with its own clear heading structure. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 08:02 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as recommended; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T08:02:38Z) | sci2audio Issue #78, PR [#144](https://github.com/dubidu22/sci2audio/pull/144); plan `plans/issue78-player-plan.md` (project files) §8 (1); D-244 |
| D-293 | 2026-11-13 | The privacy page | **`GET /privacy`**, linked from **the footer of every page** and from **the voice page next to the consent box**, states in eight plain points: (1) nothing leaves the computer: no telemetry, usage statistics, crash reports, update check, account or online service, and the voice runs here with its libraries set offline; (2) the one exception is the voice download the person starts, with consent, on the voice page; (3) FastAPI's built-in OpenTelemetry is switched off in code and tested (D-195); (4) the interface is for the person only: 127.0.0.1, a port picked per run, a key per start traded for a cookie, other pages refused, nothing loaded from outside, no access log (D-192 to D-196); (5) where the files are: working folder, audiobook folder (never replaced without "Replace these files", D-199), settings file, voice folder, and how to remove everything; (6) the cookies: the start cookie and the language cookie, both bound to 127.0.0.1; (7) what sci2audio can't control (browser and system features); (8) free software under GPL-3.0-or-later. **The download hosts and the four folders are shown live** from the running server's download plan and configuration, and a test checks each point against the code. It is a statement of how the program behaves, not a legal privacy policy. Question: what does the privacy page say and where is it linked (AQ-03)? Alternatives not chosen: a section on the start page (long text where people work; no address to link to); a static text with hosts and paths typed in (goes stale). Reason: a page that can't say what the program doesn't do. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 08:00 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as recommended; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T08:00:30Z) | sci2audio Issue #79, PR [#142](https://github.com/dubidu22/sci2audio/pull/142); plan `plans/issue79-languages-plan.md` (project files) §7, §9 (6); AQ-03; D-195, D-192 to D-199 |

On a page they are **marked `lang="en"`** (WCAG 2.2 SC 3.1.2), and the document's own words (as printed, read as, chapter titles) get the report's language. **Errors are shown by kind in the page's language** (`error.<kind>` for every kind the server, its pages or a job can send), followed by "Details from sci2audio:" and the app's words; an unknown kind shows the app's words alone. **The API is unchanged** (`{"error": kind, "message": words}`, D-197). Numbers use **the decimal sign from the table** (`common.decimal`: "." / ","; "1,25-fach", "1,2 MB") **without digit grouping**; **a no-break space** stands between a number and its unit. **Dates**, should one appear, are written as **ISO 8601** in every language. Question: how are plurals, numbers and dates handled in two languages? Whisper's own symbol spelling ("mg", "°C") is turned into words before comparing. It runs beside the existing round trip and needs no new dependency. | D-242 | 2026-11-13 | Symbols around numbers | **"%"** is left to the voice (misaki reads it right). **"‰"** after a number reads "per mille". **"≈"** reads "approximately" and **"×"** reads "times" wherever they stand, made explicit although espeak-ng says the same, as D-177 does for "≥". A doubled sign ("××") is left. **"~" and "∼"** read "about" right before a number ("~5", "τ ∼ 0.1 ps"); between operands they are left ("X ~ N(0, 1)"), and so is "5~10". **A letter "x" or "X"** reads "times" between two numbers ("3 x 4", "1920x1080", chains such as "2x3x4") or glued after one with no letter or digit after it ("10x speedup"), **except in a formula**: an operator (= + − × · / ^ < > ≤ ≥) before the number or after the x, or "(" after it, makes the x the variable ("y = 2x + 1", "3x^2", "f(x) = 4x." stay as printed); never in "0x1F", "x86", "2 x n" or a number after a hyphen ("0003-066X"). **The micro sign "µ" (U+00B5)** that is not a unit prefix becomes the Greek "μ", which the voice reads "mu" (it read "micro": "µ = 3", "µB"). Greek letters themselves stay with the voice (D-178). Question: which symbols are read, and how? Alternatives not chosen: leave ≈ and × to espeak-ng (the script and read-along would then not show what is said). Reason: measured readings; explicit where the voice relies on its fallback. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 02:37 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T02:37:00Z) | sci2audio Issue #68, PR #116; plan `plans/issue68-abbreviations-units-plan.md` (project files) §4 (P); D-177, D-178; refined after the review of PR #116, round 1 |
| D-241 | 2026-11-13 | Magnitude letters glued to a number are #68's | A **glued "K" or "k", "M", "B" or "bn"** after a number with no exponent reads "thousand", "million", "billion" ("441K apps" reads "441 thousand apps", "15M parameters", "175B tokens"), when a space, punctuation or the end follows, and no "/", "·", "^" or superscript ("5K/s" stays). A spaced "K" is kelvin; a spaced "M" is left as printed. The voice read them "K", "M", "B". D-178 did not name them; they are letters after a number, so #68 takes them. Known collisions, accepted: a glued "300K" meant as kelvin reads "300 thousand", a glued "1M" meant as molar reads "1 million" (SI style and the corpus space both). Question: who reads magnitude letters, and when? Alternatives not chosen: leave them to #67 (not planned there) or to v1.1. Reason: common in the CS papers of the corpus ("441K", "332M", "100k", "15M"). | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 02:37 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T02:37:00Z) | sci2audio Issue #68, PR #116; plan `plans/issue68-abbreviations-units-plan.md` (project files) §4 (O); D-178 |
| D-240 | 2026-11-13 | Degrees and kelvin | **"°" after a number** reads "degree"/"degrees", an edit even where the voice already says "degrees", because it said "one degrees" after 1. **"°C", "℃", "˚C", "ºC"** read "degree(s) Celsius" (the voice said "degrees C"); **"°F", "℉", "˚F", "ºF"** read "degree(s) Fahrenheit"; a **spaced "K"** reads "kelvin" (the same in the plural). "Å", "Å" and the PDF form "˚A" read "angstrom(s)" (the voice said "A ring"). Question: how are temperatures and angles read? Alternatives not chosen: leave "45°" to the voice (right except after 1). Reason: one rule gives the right plural everywhere. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 02:37 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T02:37:00Z) | sci2audio Issue #68, PR #116; plan `plans/issue68-abbreviations-units-plan.md` (project files) §4 (N); D-237 |
| D-239 | 2026-11-13 | Compound units: per, powers and products | **"/"** reads "per", each denominator its own "per" ("mg/kg/day" reads "milligrams per kilogram per day", "J/(kg·K)" "joules per kilogram per kelvin"). **Negative exponents** ("s⁻¹", "s^-1", and after another unit "s-1" or "s−1") read "per" ("m s⁻¹" reads "metres per second"; "5 s⁻¹" reads "5 per second"). **Powers 2 and 3** ("²", "³", "^2", and a plain 2 or 3 glued to a length or to "s" after "per", as PDFs print superscripts) read "square"/"cubic" before a length and "squared"/"cubed" after any other unit ("5 m²" reads "5 square metres", "m/s²" "metres per second squared"). **Products** joined by "·", "⋅" or a space are one compound when each part is in the table ("5 N m" reads "5 newton metres"); the plural goes on the last unit before "per". A single capital letter after a space is not joined unless it has an exponent ("5 m A" stays apart). "day", "week", "month" and "year" are units only after "per" or with a negative exponent ("mg kg⁻¹ day⁻¹"); "5 day" stays. An angstrom glued after another unit is a product ("3.6eV˚A" reads "3.6 electronvolt angstroms"). Question: how are compound units read? Alternatives not chosen: read "/" as "slash" (what the voice did). Reason: how scientists say them. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 02:37 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T02:37:00Z) | sci2audio Issue #68, PR #116; plan `plans/issue68-abbreviations-units-plan.md` (project files) §4 (M); D-237 |

The voice reads "metre" and "meter" the same (measured), so only the script and read-along show the spelling. Spelling by the job's language tag (en-US "meter") is sci2audio Issue #114 (v1.1). Question: which spelling? Alternatives not chosen: American spelling; by language tag now (no en-US/en-GB setting exists yet). | D-237 | 2026-11-13 | Plural agreement of units (K8) | A unit is **singular after exactly "1"** as printed (a sign before it allowed: "−1 °C" reads "−1 degree Celsius"), **plural after any other number**, "1.0", "0.5" and the end of a range ("0–1 h") included. It is singular too when the number and unit are **joined by a hyphen** ("a 5-km race" reads "a 5-kilometre race") or follow **"a" or "an"** ("a 1 m pole"). Every unit after "per" is singular. Names that do not change (hertz, kelvin, gray, the molar units) are listed with the same singular and plural. Question: when is a unit singular? Alternatives not chosen: plural for everything but "1" with no attributive rule ("a 5 kilometres race"). Reason: K8 ("1 metre", "5 kilometres per hour") and how attributive units are said. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 02:37 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T02:37:00Z) | sci2audio Issue #68, PR #116; plan `plans/issue68-abbreviations-units-plan.md` (project files) §4 (K); D-236 |
| D-236 | 2026-11-13 | Units only after a number, from an explicit table | A unit is read in full **only right after a number** as printed (digits, "," groups, a decimal part, a leading point, an exponent "10⁸"; the end of a range "1–2 h"). A unit of **one ASCII letter needs a space** after the number ("5 m", "3 h", "300 K"; never "1990s", "2D", "5G", "5m"); longer units may be glued ("9kg", "0.1ps"). The space may be a no-break, thin or narrow space or a line break. **No unit or magnitude letter is read after a label word** (Figure, Table, Section, Experiment, Group, Phase, Type, Step, ..., and their plurals: "Figure 2 A", "Table 3 C", "Figures 2B"), **nor in a list of labels after one**, joined by ",", "and", "or", "&" or a dash ("Figs. 2A and 3B", "panels A and 3B", "Tables 1A, 1B, and 2C"; "Our 7B model" still reads "7 billion"), nor after a number that ends a hyphenated name ("COVID-19 m") or follows ":" or "/" ("10:30 h", "1/2 h"). The **unit table lists every unit as printed** (93 units: length, mass, volume, time, frequency, electricity, energy, pressure, temperature, amount, sound, data, dose), with no prefixes combined automatically, so each collision is decided in the table: "in" (inch), "pm" (a time), "a", "y", "pt", "M" (molar or million), "kb" and "Mb" (kilobase or kilobit) and "um" are left out. "A" (ampere) is not read before a word in lower case, so a footnote "1 A note." keeps its article. Units the voice reads right (MHz, mol, bar) are in the table too, so plurals and the report are the same for every unit. Question: where is a unit a unit? Alternatives not chosen: prefixes combined with base units (brings in "pm", "Ms", "ks"). Reason: measured: the voice spells nearly every unit symbol. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 02:37 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T02:37:00Z) | sci2audio Issue #68, PR #116; plan `plans/issue68-abbreviations-units-plan.md` (project files) §4 (J); D-227; refined after the review of PR #116, round 1 |

| D-220 | 2026-11-13 | Progress and results on the command line | **Progress goes to stderr, the result to stdout.** On a terminal, progress is one line rewritten in place ("Speaking chapter 2, Methods: 120 of 455 sentences, about 3 min left"; "measuring the speed" until D-159's estimate exists); without a terminal (CI, a log), one line per phase and per chapter, no carriage returns. **`-q`** turns progress off; errors and the summary still print. | D-219 | 2026-11-13 | Ctrl-C on the command line | `convert` runs the audio job in a **worker thread** and waits in the main thread. **The first Ctrl-C sets the job's cancel event** (D-160): it prints "stopping after the current sentence; finished sentences are kept", the job stops at its next cancel point, and the command exits **130** with "run the same command again to continue". **A second Ctrl-C exits at once** (unfinished chunk files are removed on the next start, D-156). The main thread waits on an `Event` set by the worker, not on `Thread.join`: before Python 3.13 an interrupted `join` can report a running thread as finished (found by a test). `script` and `voices download` stop at once on Ctrl-C (their writes are atomic; the download removes its `.part` file, D-122), exit 130. Question: what does Ctrl-C do during a long conversion? Alternatives not chosen: KeyboardInterrupt wherever it lands (it could fire inside ffmpeg or a chunk write; the cancel event exists for this). Reason: a clean stop that a rerun continues. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 02:27 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T02:26:49Z) | sci2audio Issue #72, PR #108; plan `plans/issue72-cli-plan.md` (project files) §3 (10); D-160, D-156 |
| D-218 | 2026-11-13 | Resume on the command line: run the same command again | There is nothing to ask or to name: running the same `convert` again on the same file uses the same work folder, and the audio job speaks only what is missing (D-156); the summary says "Spoke N of M sentences, reused K from the last run". A stopped run leaves nothing in the output folder, so its rerun needs no `--overwrite`. A run with another voice or speed reuses nothing (D-155) and needs `--overwrite` once the earlier book is finished; the message says so. Question: how does a person resume an interrupted conversion (WI-02)? Alternatives not chosen: a `--resume` flag or a `resume` command (the cache key already makes every rerun a resume). Reason: the simplest thing that works, and nothing to remember. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 02:27 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T02:26:49Z) | sci2audio Issue #72, PR #108; plan `plans/issue72-cli-plan.md` (project files) §3 (9); D-155, D-156 |

| D-215 | 2026-11-13 | Text-step categories are switched with `--on` and `--off` | `--off CATEGORY` switches a category off (its text is read as printed); `--on CATEGORY` switches it on (sci2audio handles it: citations are left out, footnote texts are left out, D-142). Names are the `Category` values (`citations`, `footnote-texts`, ...), repeatable or comma-separated; `--help` lists every value with its label and whether it starts on. The same category in both is a usage error. Question: how does the command line expose the per-category switches (UC-02; issue #72 says "categories to leave out")? | D-214 | 2026-11-13 | The command line counts chapters from 1 | `--chapters` takes **numbers counted from 1** and ranges (`1,3-5`) or **`all`**, as the Markdown report's table and the MP3 file numbers count; the command line turns them into `JobSettings.chapters` indices (from 0). `all` reads the file once to learn how many chapters it has. A number the document lacks is a warning in the same 1-based terms and the run goes on (D-140). Question: how does the person stop a job? The OpenAPI document states each event's data exactly, so Schemathesis checks every event. Question: how does the page learn a job's progress? Alternative not chosen: `Last-Event-ID` replay (the snapshot already carries the state). Reason: D-101's SSE, kept simple. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 02:15 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T02:14:57Z) | sci2audio Issue #76, PR #107; plan `plans/issue76-server-plan.md` (project files) §12 (10); D-101 |

| D-195 | 2026-11-13 | FastAPI's built-in OpenTelemetry is switched off | FastAPI depends on `opentelemetry-api` since 0.143 and instruments requests itself. sci2audio uses the current FastAPI (**`fastapi>=0.162.5,<1`**; the telemetry test fails if a later release changes the switch, see D-206) and **switches its telemetry off in code** (`telemetry=` with tracing, metrics, logs, operation spans and environment auto-configuration all off); tests prove nothing is recorded even with a tracer provider installed and `FASTAPI_OTEL_AUTO_CONFIGURE=true` set. Without an SDK and exporter the API alone sends nothing, but sci2audio promises no telemetry. The licence notes name it, and the privacy page (#79) must too. Question: how does sci2audio stay free of telemetry with a web framework that ships it? Alternatives not chosen: pinning `fastapi<0.143` (no security fixes after July 2026); Starlette without FastAPI (no OpenAPI document, so Schemathesis and typed requests would need a hand-written schema). Reason: the no-telemetry rule, checked by tests rather than relied on. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 02:15 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T02:14:57Z) | sci2audio Issue #76, PR #107; plan `plans/issue76-server-plan.md` (project files) §12 (4); D-101; privacy page: D-293 |
| D-194 | 2026-11-13 | Request checks against other web pages | **`Host` must be `127.0.0.1:<port>` or `localhost:<port>`** (421 otherwise: DNS rebinding); **`Origin`, when sent, must be the server's own**; **`Sec-Fetch-Site` must be `same-origin` or `none`**; a **cookie-authenticated write must carry the own `Origin`** (403). No CORS. Every answer carries the full CSP of the plan's section 2.5 (`default-src 'self'; script-src 'self'; style-src 'self'; img-src 'self' data:; media-src 'self'; connect-src 'self'; frame-ancestors 'none'; base-uri 'none'; form-action 'self'`), `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer`, `X-Frame-Options: DENY`, `Cache-Control: no-store`, `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy: same-origin` and `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy: same-origin`. One pure ASGI middleware applies all of it, around errors and static files too. Question: how are cross-site requests from other pages in the same browser refused? Alternative not chosen: a CSRF form token (more moving parts; the Origin rule covers the same attacks in every browser sci2audio supports). Reason: defence in depth on top of the token (D-193). | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 02:15 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T02:14:57Z) | sci2audio Issue #76, PR #107; plan `plans/issue76-server-plan.md` (project files) §12 (3); D-193 |
| D-193 | 2026-11-13 | Start-up token and cookie | Each server run makes a **256-bit start-up token** (`secrets.token_urlsafe(32)`). The terminal prints `/start?token=…`; the browser trades it for an **HttpOnly, `SameSite=Strict` cookie named after the port** (cookies are shared between ports of one host) and is redirected to `/`, so the token leaves the address bar. Programs send `Authorization: Bearer <token>`. **Every route except `/start` needs it**, static files and the OpenAPI document included; it is valid until the server stops. Question: how does the server know a request comes from the person who started it, when any local program or web page can reach 127.0.0.1? Alternatives not chosen: the token in every URL (leaks into history and logs); a one-time token (the person could not reopen the tab from the terminal link). Reason: a local port is reachable by every program and every web page the person visits. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 02:15 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T02:14:57Z) | sci2audio Issue #76, PR #107; plan `plans/issue76-server-plan.md` (project files) §12 (2) |
| D-192 | 2026-11-13 | Server binding and port | The local server **binds 127.0.0.1 only**: no host option, IPv6 not bound. The launcher opens the socket itself on **port 0, so the system picks a free port**, and hands it to uvicorn; the start link names that port. Question: which address and port does the server listen on? Alternative not chosen: a fixed default port (bookmarkable, but it clashes with other programs and is a predictable target). Reason: the hard rule in CLAUDE.md, and nothing on the network or at a known port to aim at. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 02:15 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T02:14:57Z) | sci2audio Issue #76, PR #107; plan `plans/issue76-server-plan.md` (project files) §12 (1); D-45 |

| D-173 | 2026-11-13 | Author-year citations: removed in brackets, names kept where the sentence needs them (TP-03, TP-04, K2) | **A citation part** is optional lead words ("see", "e.g.,", "for a review see"), **authors** (names joined by ",", "and", "&", each maybe with "et al."; a name starts with a capital, with diacritics, hyphens, apostrophes and particles such as van, de, der allowed; an all-capital word of 2 to 6 letters only before a comma, "WHO, 2020"; month names and mixed-case words like "CoNLL" never), then **years** (1500 to 2099 with letters "2016a,b", "in press", "n.d.", "forthcoming"), after a comma or in their own brackets, then optional **locators** (", p. 8", ", Chapter 9", ", Sec. 2", ", inter alia"). A bracket's parts are split at ";" (and at "," between parts that end in a bracketed year). Rules: (1) **every part a citation**: the bracket goes ("(Smith et al., 2019; Jones & Lee, 2020)."); (2) **some parts**: only those go, the rest stays ("(RNNGs; Dyer et al., 2016)" reads "(RNNGs)"); (3) **used as the subject** (the bracket starts a sentence and a lower-case word follows) or as a noun after a preposition ("such as (Qin et al., 2022)"): **the names stay** ("Dasigi et al. note that"); (4) **narrative**: a bracket of only years and locators right after a name or "et al.": **only the bracket goes** ("Smith (2019) shows" reads "Smith shows"); (5) a **page bracket glued** to a citation ("(2022)[p. 8]") goes with it; one after a closing quotation mark goes on its own. Brackets are worked **outermost first**: a bracket that goes takes everything inside it along, and inside one that stays, its inner brackets are looked at in turn (the plan said innermost first; the results on the corpus are the same). A removal never leaves two marks side by side ("Zimmeck et al. (2019). MAPS" reads "Zimmeck et al. MAPS"; review of sci2audio PR #103, M2). "et al." stays as printed for #68. Question: how are author-year citations read (TP-03 asks to drop asides, TP-04 to keep what the sentence needs)? Alternatives not chosen: the old app's spoken note ("Sources: Smith and colleagues, twenty nineteen"; makes every paragraph longer); removing narrative citations whole (the sentence loses its subject). Reason: the sentence stays whole and the listener hears who said it where the text names them. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 01:53 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:53:29Z) | sci2audio Issue #66, PR #103; plan `plans/issue66-citations-urls-plan.md` (project files) §1.4, §3 C3; audit K2 |
| D-172 | 2026-11-13 | Numeric citations: removed as asides, read as "reference 12" where the sentence needs them (TP-03, TP-04) | By the first rule that applies: (1) a bracket holding **only lead words and numeric citations** ("(see also [48, 135])", "(e.g., [3])") **goes whole**; (2) a citation that is **part of the sentence** (after a preposition or link word such as in, by, of, from, see, with, than, like, cf., e.g.; after "Ref.", "Refs.", "Reference" or "References" (the last two capitalised), replaced along ("in Reference [12]" reads "in reference 12"; review of sci2audio PR #103, R2-3); or starting a sentence, block or bracket) is **read as "reference 12"**, "references 3 and 5", "references 2 to 6", "references 8 to 10, 16 and 44", one `replace` edit ("discussed in [26]." reads "discussed in reference 26."; "Refs. [53, 58]" reads "references 53 and 58"), with a capital only at a real sentence start, not after an abbreviation ("cf. reference 4"; review of sci2audio PR #103, S3); digits stay for the number rules (#67); the words come from the wording table (D-145); (3) **otherwise it is an aside and goes** with the white space before it ("remain inconsistent [2–6, 16, 17]." reads "remain inconsistent."), also after "and so on" or "and so forth". Question: what does the listener hear for a numeric citation? Alternatives not chosen: removing the preposition with it (changes the meaning: "similar to [12]." would read "similar."); always removing (leaves "as shown by ."); the bare number ("as shown by 12"). Reason: asides go, and a sentence that points to a source still says so. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 01:53 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:53:29Z); **extended by D-447** (13 Nov): lead words with a narrative author-year citation are read whole too | sci2audio Issue #66, PR #103; plan `plans/issue66-citations-urls-plan.md` (project files) §1.3, §3 C2; D-145 |
| D-171 | 2026-11-13 | What a numeric citation is | A numeric citation is **a square bracket holding only whole numbers from 1 to 9999** (one optional letter, "12a"), separated by "," or ";", and **ranges** with "-", "–" or "—", white space and line breaks allowed. **Never a citation:** a bracket glued to a letter, digit or closing bracket before it ("Tr[ρ]", "x[1]"); one after a maths or statistics sign or word (signs such as `= ∈ → < > ≤ ≥ ×`, words "CI", "interval", "range": "∈ [0, 1]", "CI [1, 3]"); one after a **capitalised** label word such as "Eq.", "Fig.", "Table", "Section", "Step", "Algorithm" ("Eq. [3]" is an equation number, while "the EM algorithm [5]" and "the first step [7]" stay citations; review of sci2audio PR #103, S3 and R2-2); one holding 0, a decimal point or another letter ("3e-4"); round brackets ("(1)" is an equation or list number). **Superscript numbers (Nature style) are not covered in v1.0**: without the reference list they cannot be told from footnote marks; follow-up sci2audio Issue #100. Question: which brackets are citations, and which are maths? Alternative not chosen: also superscript numbers (would remove footnote marks the footnote step reads). Reason: on the corpus papers the rule finds every numeric citation outside the reference lists and no maths. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 01:53 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:53:29Z) | sci2audio Issue #66, PR #103; plan `plans/issue66-citations-urls-plan.md` (project files) §1.3, §3 C1; Issue #100 |
| D-170 | 2026-11-13 | Addresses broken across lines are joined | A URL that reaches a line break **goes on in the next line** when it ends in `/ - _ ~ = & ? # %`, in `:` after the scheme ("https:\n//doi.org"), or in "." when the next line's first word starts with a lower-case letter or digit and holds a "/" or only digits ("github.\ncom/citp"); otherwise it ends at the break. Closing punctuation at the end (`. , ; : ! ? ' "` and an unbalanced `)` or `]`) belongs to the sentence. The `links` edit covers every line break inside the address, **a break at a hyphen included** ("2023.acl-\nshort.4"), so the line-end hyphen step (D-149, D-150) is set aside there (review of sci2audio PR #98, N1). | D-168 | 2026-11-13 | Links are removed and the sentence repaired (TP-07) | URLs (`http://`, `https://`, `ftp://`, `www.`, a bare `host.tld/path` with a known top-level domain), DOIs (`doi:`, `DOI:`, a bare `10.NNNN/...`), arXiv identifiers (D-169) and e-mail addresses (also `mailto:` and `{a, b}@host` groups) are removed; neighbouring links joined by ",", ";", "/", "and", "or", "&" are one edit. The first repair rule that applies decides what goes along, always with the white space before it: (1) **a bracket holding only the link** and lead words ("(see https://...)", "(available at ...)") goes whole, and a preposition before it too when a comma or stop follows ("shown in [https://...], it" reads "shown, it"); (2) **a label** ("Contact:", "E-mail:", "Code:", "Code: see") goes with it, and the whole sentence when the label starts it and, links left out, nothing but join and pointer words follow the link ("Contact: a@b.org or see https://c.org/x." goes whole); (3) **another colon** before it goes; (4) **a preposition or verb** before it goes ("released at https://..." reads "released"); a **sentence or bracket that a pointer word ("See", "See also", "Visit", "cf.") starts** goes whole when after the link it holds nothing, only more links and their join words, or a short purpose ("for", "to", "about", "regarding": "See https://... for details."; at most six words, no clause); otherwise only the pointer and the link go; (5) otherwise only the link goes. A comma or semicolon left before ".", ",", ";" or a closing bracket goes too ("(Smith, 2019, https://...)" reads "(Smith, 2019)"). A removal that starts a sentence takes the mark after it, not the space before ("Foo. https://x.org/a. Bar" reads "Foo. Bar"), and a comma or semicolon that several removals leave before ".", "!" or "?" goes. A repair that reaches over a later link takes it into the same edit, so the step never proposes overlapping edits. **A block or footnote holding nothing but links** (and its mark) reads nothing, so no lone "Footnote 5." is left. These refinements of the plan's five rules were added in the code PR, after probing and in its review round 1 (M1, M2, S1), within this decision's aim; the plan had listed "see https://... for details" as an accepted leftover. Known leftover: a link inside a bracket that also holds a citation is removed first, so the citation is read (sci2audio Issue #102). Question: how is a sentence read once its link is gone? Alternatives not chosen: reading the host name ("released at github dot com"; the issue asks for removal); a notice "A link is skipped." (fills the audio with notices). Reason: the sentence reads as if the link had never been printed. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 01:53 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:53:29Z) | sci2audio Issue #66, PR #103; plan `plans/issue66-citations-urls-plan.md` (project files) §1.2, §3 L2; Issue #102 |

In the registry (D-105) they come **right after the block steps, `links` first**, before the footnote marks (#64), numbers and abbreviations (#67, #68) and the character steps (D-150): a URL can hold things that look like citations or numbers, and an address removed whole must not be cut up by a later rule. Both skip furniture, formula, table and figure blocks and never ask. Question: one step or two, for which languages, and where in the order? Alternatives not chosen: one step for both (one switch would turn off two categories); an any-language links step (its repair words are English).
