| D-166 | 2026-11-13 | The voice is passed to the audio job as a `VoiceSource` | The audio job (#71) gets its voice as **`VoiceSource(engine, version, make, voices, default_voice, release)`**: the engine's name and version (for the chunk key, D-155), a function that makes the engine, the voice ids it has (checked before any work), its default voice, and a function that hands the engine back when the job ends (default: dispose it). `kokoro_voice(models_dir)` builds the Kokoro one; the models folder lives in the source, not in the job's arguments. **The engine is made only when a chunk has to be spoken**, so a rerun whose chunks are all cached never loads the model. Question: how does the job get its voice without loading a model it may not need? Alternatives not chosen: an engine object passed in (the model would load even for a fully cached rerun); the job creating Kokoro itself (tests and a later server could not pass their own). Reason: testable with a fake voice, cheap reruns, and a server can later keep one engine loaded by passing its own `release`. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 01:46 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:46:19Z); **used by D-250** (13 Nov: the server keeps one engine loaded) | sci2audio Issue #71, PR #99; plan `plans/issue71-audio-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (13); D-125, D-122 |
| D-165 | 2026-11-13 | The audio job's records: `audio.json` and `document.json` | The audio job writes two records to its working folder, **after** the audiobook: **`audio.json`** (`"format": "sci2audio-audio"`, `"version": 1`): sci2audio version, source name, format and SHA-256, the settings (as in `job.json`), engine, engine version, voice and speed, the M4B's duration, per chapter whether chosen, its chunk count and seconds, the counts of chunks spoken, reused and skipped, every skipped utterance (chapter, block, span, page, printed page, text, error), the measured characters per second, the size of the chunk cache and the names of the files written; and **`document.json`**, the laid-out `Document` through `core.export.to_data`, so the read-along player (#78) can show the block text the read-along spans point at without the source file (about 1 MB for the largest corpus paper). The script job's `job.json` (D-137) stays as it is. Question: what does the audio job leave behind, and how does the player get the block text? Alternatives not chosen: extending `job.json` (two jobs would write one file); letting the player run extraction and layout again (needs the source file and the same code version). Reason: a small, versioned record per job, and the player's input kept next to the job. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 01:46 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:46:19Z) | sci2audio Issue #71, PR #99; plan `plans/issue71-audio-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (12); D-137, D-102 |
| D-164 | 2026-11-13 | Default folders for the voice files and the audio job's working folders | For the command line and the server only (a job always gets its folders passed in): **voice files in `platformdirs.user_data_path("sci2audio")/models`** (the user's data folder, not the cache, so a system clean-up never deletes the 355 MB download), and **one working folder per source file in `platformdirs.user_cache_path("sci2audio")/jobs/<first 16 hex digits of the file's SHA-256>`**, so the same file under another name or in another folder finds its chunks again. `app.default_models_folder()` and `app.default_work_folder(source)`. D-122 left the models folder to the app. Question: where do the voice files and the chunk cache live by default? Alternatives not chosen: the models folder in the cache folder (cleaners may delete it); a working folder next to the source file (clutters the person's folders, and read-only folders fail). Reason: platform conventions through the platformdirs dependency we already have (D-139). | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 01:46 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:46:19Z) | sci2audio Issue #71, PR #99; plan `plans/issue71-audio-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (11); D-122, D-139 |
| D-163 | 2026-11-13 | The audio job checks for an existing audiobook before any speech | Without `overwrite`, the audio job **computes the audiobook's file names with output's naming rules and stops with `OutputExistsError` before the voice speaks** if any of them exists; output checks again when it writes (D-119). Question: when does a job that would replace files find out? Alternative not chosen: relying on output's own check (it runs after the speech, possibly an hour later). Reason: a refusal should cost nothing, as in the script job (D-136). | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 01:46 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:46:19Z) | sci2audio Issue #71, PR #99; plan `plans/issue71-audio-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (10); D-119, D-136 |
| D-162 | 2026-11-13 | When the voice fails on a sentence | If the voice fails on one utterance (`EngineError` from `synthesize`), that utterance is **left out of the audiobook and listed** in the result and in `audio.json` with its chapter, page, text and error, and the job goes on; a skipped chunk's pause carries over to the next chunk. **After 5 failures in a row** the voice is taken as broken and the job stops with a `JobError`; the chunks spoken so far stay for a rerun. Missing voice files (`ModelNotFoundError`) stop the job before any speech with "the voice is not set up". If nothing at all could be spoken, the job fails. Question: does one sentence the voice can't say stop the whole book? Alternative not chosen: stopping on the first failure (one odd sentence would block the book for good, since a rerun fails the same way). Reason: D-61 (3)'s never-stop rule and D-140, with a clear stop when the voice itself is broken. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 01:46 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:46:19Z) | sci2audio Issue #71, PR #99; plan `plans/issue71-audio-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (9); D-140, D-61 |
| D-161 | 2026-11-13 | One audio job per working folder, across processes | While an audio job runs it holds an **operating-system lock on `<work>/job.lock`** (`fcntl.flock` on Linux and macOS, `msvcrt.locking` on Windows, standard library only); a second job on the same folder, in this or another process, gets `JobBusyError`. The system releases the lock when the process ends, even when it is killed, so no stale lock is left. This comes **on top of** the runner's lock (D-138). Question: how are two processes (command line and server, two windows) kept off one working folder? Alternatives not chosen: a lock file holding a process id (stale after a kill, needs a liveness check per system); the `filelock` package (a new dependency for about 30 lines). Reason: the simplest lock that is correct after a crash. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 01:46 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:46:19Z) | sci2audio Issue #71, PR #99; plan `plans/issue71-audio-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (8); D-138 |
| D-160 | 2026-11-13 | Cancelling an audio job | The audio job takes a **`threading.Event`**; it is checked **before each utterance** (one voice call takes at most a few seconds) and **between the blocks of audio fed to ffmpeg** (through a thin wrapper around output's `Ffmpeg`, so output removes its partial file, D-119). A cancelled job raises **`JobCancelledError`** (a `JobError`); the finished chunks stay for a rerun and no audiobook file is left half-written. Question: how is a long job stopped at any time (#71)? Alternative not chosen: killing the job's thread or process (no clean-up, and Python cannot stop a thread from outside). Reason: a quick, clean stop that keeps the work done. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 01:46 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:46:19Z) | sci2audio Issue #71, PR #99; plan `plans/issue71-audio-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (7); D-119 |
| D-159 | 2026-11-13 | Progress events and the measured time estimate (WI-04) | The audio job reports **`AudioProgress`** as each phase starts (reading, layout, text steps, loading the voice, speaking, encoding, done: `Phase` gains the three audio phases) and **after every chunk**: chapter, chunks done and total, chunks reused, characters spoken and still to speak in this run, characters per second, seconds left. **The speed is measured on this run's own speech**: characters spoken divided by the time spent speaking, without the model load and without the first call (warm-up); cached chunks don't count. **The estimate (`seconds_left`) appears once the first chapter with speech is finished and at least 500 characters are measured**, then it is recalculated after every chunk from all speech measured so far. Encoding time is not estimated (seconds; shown as its own phase). Question: when and how is the time estimate measured, as playbook 3.2 asks ("on the first chapter, not a fixed rate")? Alternative not chosen: the first chapter alone (the ACL papers' front chapter holds only about 200 characters, too few to measure). Reason: a measured, honest estimate that improves as the job runs. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 01:46 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:46:19Z); **partly revised by D-380 to D-382** (13 Nov): a per-chunk cost model replaces remaining characters divided by speed, the first 4 voice calls are warm-up, the first estimate waits for 1,000 characters | sci2audio Issue #71, PR #99; plan `plans/issue71-audio-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (6); playbook 3.2 |
| D-158 | 2026-11-13 | Chunks stay after a finished job | The chunk cache stays in the working folder after the audiobook is written, so a rerun after changing a switch or the chapter choice speaks only what changed. **`clean_work_folder(work)`** removes the cache (not the records); the command line (#72) and the interface (#77) offer it, and `audio.json` states the cache's size (about 173 MB per hour of audio). Question: does the job delete its chunks when it succeeds? Alternative not chosen: deleting them on success (a switch change would speak the whole book again). Reason: the report-and-switches workflow (UC-01, UC-02) makes reruns common; the person decides when to free the space. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 01:46 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:46:19Z) | sci2audio Issue #71, PR #99; plan `plans/issue71-audio-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (5) |
| D-157 | 2026-11-13 | Utterances mark where the length limit cut a sentence (refines D-115) | textprep's `Utterance` gains **`continues: bool = False`**, set by `cut_utterances` on every utterance after the first of a sentence the length limit cut (D-115 (4)). The audio job gives such an utterance **`Pause.NONE`** (D-116), and the next sentence `Pause.SENTENCE`. The default keeps every caller working. Question: how does the app tell a clause cut from a sentence end, which D-116 pause differently? Alternative not chosen: guessing from the previous utterance's last character (misreads "e.g." cuts and replacements ending in a full stop). Reason: the cutter knows; one field passes it on. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 01:46 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:46:19Z) | sci2audio Issue #71, PR #99; plan `plans/issue71-audio-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (4); D-115, D-116 |
| D-156 | 2026-11-13 | Resume per chunk; every chapter is encoded again (WI-02) | **Resuming is running the audio job again on the same working folder**: every finished chunk is used again, so a chapter whose chunks all exist needs no speech. A chunk is finished when its WAV file is complete (16-bit mono PCM, as many bytes as its header says); the voice writes `.part` and renames (`voices.write_wav`), so a killed run leaves at most a `.part` file, which the next run removes. **All chapters are encoded again at the end**, so output stays unchanged. Question: what does "skip finished chunks and chapters" mean for the files? Alternative not chosen: keeping finished chapter MP3s (an output change to save seconds: encoding takes seconds per chapter, speech minutes per page). Reason: one simple rule, proven by a kill-and-resume test. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 01:46 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:46:19Z) | sci2audio Issue #71, PR #99; plan `plans/issue71-audio-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (3); Issue #10 |
| D-155 | 2026-11-13 | The chunk cache key | A chunk is cached as **`chunks/<first 2 hex>/<key>.wav`** in the working folder, where the key is the **SHA-256 of a canonical JSON of the text, the voice, the speed, the engine and the engine version**; for Kokoro the version is the pinned model revision plus the installed kokoro and misaki versions (`kokoro-82m@f3ff3571791e;kokoro 0.9.4;misaki 0.9.4`). **The span is not in the key**, so the same text twice in a job ("Table 2 is skipped.") is spoken once. Question: what decides that a cached chunk can be used again (#71: "text, voice, speed and engine version")? Alternatives not chosen: a key per utterance position (the same text spoken many times; every edit upstream would invalidate everything after it); without the misaki version (a new phonemizer can change the sound). Reason: exactly what decides the sound, nothing else. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 01:46 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:46:19Z) | sci2audio Issue #71, PR #99; plan `plans/issue71-audio-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (2) |
| D-154 | 2026-11-13 | What the audio job writes | The audio job (#71) writes **only the audiobook files** into the output folder (one MP3 per chapter, the M4B, the read-along file; D-102, D-119) and its records into the working folder (D-165). It prepares the listening script **in memory**, exactly as the script job does, so the same file and settings give the same utterances, but it does **not** write the script or the report; the command line `convert` (#72) and the interface run the script job first when they want them. Question: does the audio job also write the script and report? Alternative not chosen: writing them too (a `script` run followed by a conversion would then need `overwrite` for files the person may have read or edited). Reason: two jobs, two sets of files, no overwrite conflicts. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 13 Nov 01:46 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:46:19Z) | sci2audio Issue #71, PR #99; plan `plans/issue71-audio-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (1); D-137 |
| D-153 | 2026-11-12 | TeX accents set apart from their letter are cleaned up (IN-04) | PDFs set in TeX often give an accent as a separate spacing character before its letter: "Garc´ıa", "G¨obel", "˚Akerman", "Hovanˇc´ık" (20 places in the corpus papers). The `unicode-clean-up` text step **replaces an accent (´ ¨ ˚ ˇ ˜ ¯ ˘ ˙ ¸ ˛ ˝, and ˆ only inside a word) and the Latin letter right after it by the composed letter** when the two compose to one character (ı and ȷ count as i and j), and **removes an accent that stands alone between white space** ("Marasovic, ´ Swabha"). An acute used as an apostrophe ("it´s") and hats in maths ("ˆj", "Lˆ") are left alone. Question: issue #65 names ligatures, soft hyphens, odd spaces and math letters; are set-apart accents in scope? Alternatives not chosen: leaving them (the voice stumbles on every such name); a separate issue (the same kind of Unicode clean-up, in the same step). Reason: names are read in body text and citations; the fix is small and marked like every other edit. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:36 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:36:27Z) | sci2audio Issue #65, PR #98; plan `plans/issue65-unicode-hyphens-plan.md` (project files) §3 U5 |
| D-152 | 2026-11-12 | Line-end hyphens: English only in v1.0 | The `line-end-hyphens` text step runs for **English only** in v1.0; the `unicode-clean-up` step runs for every language. German needs its own rule: other words after a suspended hyphen ("und", "oder", "bis": "Wohnungs-/und") and no capital-letter test, since German nouns start with a capital. Follow-up: sci2audio Issue #96 (milestone v1.1). Question: which languages does the hyphen rule serve in v1.0? Alternatives not chosen: running the English rule on German (it would join "Wohnungs- und" and keep every hyphen before a noun). Reason: v1.0 is English first; a wrong German rule is worse than none. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:36 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:36:27Z) | sci2audio Issue #65, PR #98; plan `plans/issue65-unicode-hyphens-plan.md` (project files) §3 U4 |
| D-151 | 2026-11-12 | No text edits for white space | No-break, thin, narrow and other Unicode spaces get **no edits**: `spoken_text` and the utterance cutter already read every run of white space as one plain space (D-98), so an edit would change nothing a listener hears. A regression case (`odd-spaces`) pins this, which meets issue #65's criterion that odd spaces become plain text. Invisible characters that are not white space (zero-width space, word joiner, byte order mark, bidirectional marks, invisible maths operators) are removed by `remove` edits; ZWJ and ZWNJ stay, as emoji and some scripts need them. Question: do odd spaces need edits of their own? Alternatives not chosen: one `replace` edit per odd space (fills the report with edits that change nothing, the reason D-98 gave for line breaks; and such one-character edits would block later rules over the same text). Reason: one rule for white space, in one place. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:36 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:36:27Z) | sci2audio Issue #65, PR #98; plan `plans/issue65-unicode-hyphens-plan.md` (project files) §3 U3 |
| D-150 | 2026-11-12 | Characters and hyphenation run last among the inline steps (refines D-105) | In the fixed registry (D-105), the two steps of the category Characters and hyphenation (D-108), `unicode-clean-up` and `line-end-hyphens`, are **the last inline steps**: after URLs, citations, footnote marks, numbers, units, statistics and abbreviations. The plan of #63 had placed them before numbers and abbreviations. Every step sees the original text and the earlier step wins an overlap, so a one-character fix placed early would set aside a later rule covering the same text: a ligature fix in "ﬁg." would block the abbreviation step, a math-letter fix in "𝑝 < .05" the statistics step. Placed last, these fixes apply only where no other rule rewrote the text, and every other rule writes plain text itself. A test checks that the two steps are last. Question: where in the inline order do character fixes go? Alternatives not chosen: first among the inline steps (blocks later rules); the order of the #63 plan (blocks numbers, units and abbreviations). Reason: the coarse-to-fine rule of D-105 applied to character fixes, the finest edits of all. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:36 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:36:27Z) | sci2audio Issue #65, PR #98; plan `plans/issue65-unicode-hyphens-plan.md` (project files) §3 U2 |
| D-149 | 2026-11-12 | Line-end hyphens without a word list | The `line-end-hyphens` step decides **without a dictionary or word list**, by one written rule (in the module `sci2audio.textprep.hyphens`): a hyphen next to a digit or mark is kept; before "and", "or", "nor" it is a suspended hyphen and the line break is read as a space; otherwise **the document's own spelling decides** (the form it prints more often outside line ends, joined or hyphenated; a tie keeps the hyphen); then compound signals (one letter before, a capital on either side, a hyphen already in the word) and two short lists ("self" before; based, related, specific and similar after) keep the hyphen; otherwise the word is joined. On the 495 line-end hyphens of the seven corpus papers the rule reads 488 as a careful reader would; the 7 misses are listed in the hand-checked truth file `tests/regression/line-end-hyphens-en.toml`. Question: does TP-02 need a word list, as the old app had (wordfreq)? Alternatives not chosen: wordfreq (data licence CC BY-SA 4.0, outside the licence gate's list, and about 20 MB of dependencies); a word list derived from SCOWL (a permissive licence without an SPDX identifier, about 500 KB) for a gain of about 1.4% on the corpus. Reason: the document's own spelling settles 374 of the 495 cases, no licence question, nothing to download. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:36 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:36:27Z) | sci2audio Issue #65, PR #98; plan `plans/issue65-unicode-hyphens-plan.md` (project files) §3 U1 |
| D-148 | 2026-11-12 | Regression cases: superscript runs and sections | The English regression format (D-113) gains two optional keys. **A block may give `runs` in place of `text`**: a list of strings and `{ sup = "1" }` tables, joined into the block's text, each `sup` a run of its own tagged `sup` (as an EPUB gives a superscript, or as a PDF mark set small). **A case may give `sections`**, a flat list of `{ title, level, start, end }`. `sci2audio.testing.make_document` gains the matching keyword arguments `tags` (character ranges that become tagged runs) and `sections`, and the conformance suite's sample paper gains a superscript footnote mark and a References section, so every built-in step is checked on work it really does. Question: how do regression cases express what the footnote-mark and reference-list rules look at (run styles and the section tree), which the format of D-113 cannot? Alternatives not chosen: a markup inside the text such as `^{1}` (could clash with LaTeX in later Markdown cases, and the block text would no longer be what the case shows); unit tests only, no regression cases for these rules (tests.md asks for a regression case before every text rule). Reason: the format stays readable and mirrors core's `TextRun.tags`; existing cases are unchanged. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:35 UTC, under D-80; plan approved with option A for its decision 1; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:35:34Z); **extended by D-426** (13 Nov): `{ text, tags }` runs and a block key `page` | sci2audio Issue #64, PR #94; plan `plans/issue64-placeholders-footnotes-plan.md` (project files) §6 (7); D-113, D-143, D-146 |
| D-147 | 2026-11-12 | The #64 steps are English only, except "Footnote texts" | The placeholder notices (`formula-notices`, `table-figure-notices`), the reference list (`reference-list`) and the footnote marks and introductions (`footnotes`) declare **`languages = ("en",)`**: their words come from the wording table (D-145) and their names from English lists (D-146), and a job in another language reports them as not run. **`footnote-texts` declares any language** (`*`): it only removes notes and says nothing. Question: which languages do the new steps claim? Alternatives not chosen: any language with English words (an English notice in a German audiobook); English for `footnote-texts` too (the switch would do nothing for other languages for no reason). Reason: v1.0 reads English (D-50, Q10); a second language adds a wording row and a name list, then widens the steps. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:35 UTC, under D-80; plan approved with option A for its decision 1; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:35:34Z) | sci2audio Issue #64, PR #94; plan `plans/issue64-placeholders-footnotes-plan.md` (project files) §6 (6); D-50, D-145, D-146 |
| D-146 | 2026-11-12 | The reference list is removed by its section title; lists without a heading are deferred | The step `reference-list` (category Reference list, block stage) finds **every section, at any level of the section tree, whose title is a usual name of a reference list** (`textprep.is_reference_title`). The names are `textprep.REFERENCE_TITLES`: **References, Bibliography, Reference list, Works cited, Literature cited**, D-83's two names plus the rest of D-132's English list. A title matches when, with each run of white space read as one space, **an optional printed number** (one or two digits, one capital letter, or one to five of the Roman letters I, V and X; then any subnumbers of one or two digits, each after a full stop; then an optional full stop; then white space) is taken off and what is left is one of the names **in any case, without any closing "." or ":"**. So "References", "REFERENCES", "References:", "7 References", "7. References", "8.1 Bibliography", "A.1 References", "IV. Bibliography" and "Works Cited" match; "Further references", "References and notes", "Notes" and "100 References" do not. The app's chapter choice treats titles the same way: its English names (`app.chapters.REFERENCE_NAMES["en"]`) are this tuple, imported from textprep, and its `is_reference_title` takes off the same number and closing marks; a test runs both on one shared list of titles and requires the same answer for each (PR #94, commit b76826e). This does what issue #95 asked for English; the German chapter names stay in the app until a second language comes (#79). It proposes **one `remove` per block with text in that section, the heading included**; **footnote blocks are kept** (their marks stand in the body; Arsiwalla's note 19 is printed at the foot of the page where the list starts) and **furniture is left to the furniture step**. The "Reference list" switch keeps the list. **A reference list printed without a heading** (PRL style, the corpus paper Liu) is not found; sci2audio issue #92 tracks it. Question: how does TP-06 recognise the reference list, and what does it leave in place? Alternatives not chosen: D-83's two names only, as the plan first said (a "Works cited" chapter would be deselected by the app but read by the step); removing notes inside the section (would drop a body note printed on that page); detecting unheaded lists now (needs a list detector, not a title rule). Reason: the printed heading is how papers mark the list, and a chapter the app deselects as the reference list must be the one the step leaves out. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:35 UTC, under D-80; plan approved with option A for its decision 1; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:35:34Z; the widening to D-132's five names decided by the coordinator on 13 Nov 01:40 UTC under D-80, Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-13T01:40:37Z) | sci2audio Issue #64, PR #94; plan `plans/issue64-placeholders-footnotes-plan.md` (project files) §6 (5); D-83, D-132, TP-06; issues #92, #95 |
| D-145 | 2026-11-12 | Placeholder notices: named by every linked caption, one wording table | Each table, figure and formula block is **replaced by one short notice** (block stage, its whole text; an empty block gets the notice inserted). **Names come from the labels of every caption whose `describes` points at the block, in document order, as printed**, with label words printed short read in full (Fig. → Figure, Figs. → Figures, Tab. → Table, Eq. → Equation, Alg. → Algorithm), so "Algorithm 1, Algorithm 2 and Figure 1 are skipped." (Nowak's figure block holds both algorithms and the figure); **without a caption a formula is named by its own printed number** ("Formula 16a is skipped."); without either: "A table is skipped.", "A figure is skipped.", "A formula is skipped.". The caption is never repeated and reads as printed in its place (Q3). **Every word the #64 steps add lives in one table, `textprep/wording.py`, keyed by primary language**; only English exists (D-50), German follows with #79. Formulas (`formula-notices`) and tables and figures (`table-figure-notices`) are two steps, one per category (D-108). Question: how is a placeholder named when several captions describe it or the label is printed short, and where does the wording live (Q3 left both to the plan)? Alternatives not chosen: only the first caption (Nowak's notice would name one of three items); the label exactly as printed ("Fig. 3 is skipped." read with a spoken abbreviation); wording in each step (a translation would touch every step). Reason: the listener hears every item that is skipped, in plain words, and a later language is one table row. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:35 UTC, under D-80; plan approved with option A for its decision 1; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:35:34Z) | sci2audio Issue #64, PR #94; plan `plans/issue64-placeholders-footnotes-plan.md` (project files) §6 (4); D-55, D-108, Q3 |
| D-144 | 2026-11-12 | Footnote introductions: "Footnote 1.", "Footnote.", never twice | The step `footnotes` introduces each note where layout placed it (Q2) with **one `replace` of its leading mark: "Footnote 1." for a mark of digits or letters**, and **"Footnote." for a symbol mark** (∗ † ‡ §: a listener cannot match a symbol, and the marks are gone from the running text). **A note without a mark at its start gets "Footnote. " inserted** before its first character. A space is added after the introduction when the note's text follows the mark directly ("1We"). **A note whose text already starts with the word "Footnote" gets nothing**, so the step is idempotent; an empty or blank note gets nothing. Question: what exactly introduces a note (Q2 named only "Footnote 1.")? Alternatives not chosen: speaking the symbol ("Footnote asterisk"); no introduction for an unmarked note (the listener could not tell it from body text). Reason: every note is set apart the same short way, and the step can run on its own output. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:35 UTC, under D-80; plan approved with option A for its decision 1; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:35:34Z); **refined for tagged files by D-422 and D-425** (13 Nov): a note with label "" is introduced by its first linked mark | sci2audio Issue #64, PR #94; plan `plans/issue64-placeholders-footnotes-plan.md` (project files) §6 (3); Q2, TP-05 |
| D-143 | 2026-11-12 | Footnote marks: four conditions, at most one mark per label per page | The step `footnotes` removes a footnote mark from a paragraph, heading, caption or list item **only when all four hold**: (1) the run is **tagged `sup` by the source or set more than 8 % smaller than its block's main size** (layout's `size_change`); (2) its text is **the label of a footnote printed on the same page**; (3) it **follows a word ending in at least two letters** (digits may follow, as in "Ahmad3" before an asterisk mark) **or closing punctuation**; (4) **the next character that is not white space is not a maths operator** (+ = − × · \| < > ≤ ≥ ∈ ∉ ⊂ ⊆ ∑ ∏ / ^ _). **Each label is removed at most once per page**, at its first match in reading order. A note whose mark is not found is still read, introduced (D-144). On the 7 corpus papers the rule finds the marks of all 39 labelled notes and nothing else; the maths exponents in Nowak's abstract ("O(N \|N \|3 + …)") stay; a golden file lists every removed mark with its context. Question: how is a footnote mark told apart from a maths superscript or another small number? Alternatives not chosen: any small run matching a label (removed Nowak's exponents and so missed the real marks 2 and 3 on that page); a mark of a note anywhere in the document (more false hits; notes stand on their mark's page). Reason: measured on the whole corpus with no miss and no false hit, from what extraction records, without a core change. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:35 UTC, under D-80; plan approved with option A for its decision 1; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:35:34Z); **refined for tagged files by D-420 to D-428** (13 Nov): marks found by link tags, every linked mark removed | sci2audio Issue #64, PR #94; plan `plans/issue64-placeholders-footnotes-plan.md` (project files) §6 (2); Q2, TP-05; issue #93 (author-affiliation marks) |
| D-142 | 2026-11-12 | An 11th text-step category, "Footnote texts", off by default (refines D-108 and D-110) | Q2 says one switch leaves all footnotes out, but by D-110 switching the Footnotes category off only drops its own edits (marks removed, notes introduced), which reads marks and notes as printed. So **a new category `footnote-texts`, labelled "Footnote texts", holds one block step, `footnote-texts`, that removes every footnote block; it is the one category that is off by default**: `Category.on_by_default` is false only for it, `textprep.DEFAULT_SWITCHED_OFF` is `{footnote-texts}`, and **`run_steps` takes `switched_off=DEFAULT_SWITCHED_OFF` by default**, so the app's job settings (#70) start from it. Switching it on leaves every note out; its removals claim the notes' blocks (D-128), so the introductions are set aside and named in the report with the removal that won, and the marks stay removed. "Footnotes" stays on by default; switched off, marks and notes are read as printed, like every other category. D-108's list grows from ten to eleven. Question: how can one switch leave all footnotes out when a switched-off category only drops its own edits (plan decision 1)? Alternatives not chosen: (B) a per-step switch with a `default_on` step attribute (a larger change to the interface, the runner and the interface's switches); (C) a "read footnotes" job setting in #70 that drops footnote blocks when utterances are cut (footnote handling split over two layers, the left-out notes not edits, Q2's category switch made a job setting). Reason: every left-out note stays a marked edit with a reason (D-57), D-110 keeps its meaning, and the change is one enum member and one default. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:35 UTC, under D-80; plan approved with option A for its decision 1; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:35:34Z) | sci2audio Issue #64, PR #94; plan `plans/issue64-placeholders-footnotes-plan.md` (project files) §6 (1); Q2, D-108, D-110, D-128 |
| D-141 | 2026-11-12 | Voice download and spaCy path: three refinements from the review of PR #88 (refines D-121, D-122) | Found in review round 1 of sci2audio PR #88 (the Kokoro voice, Issue #18). **(a) The spaCy folder goes to the end of `sys.path`, not the front** (refines D-121, which said "at the front"): `voices.prepare_environment()` appends `<models folder>/spacy/en_core_web_sm-3.8.0/` once. spaCy still finds the model (`spacy.util.is_package` searches every entry), and a folder the user can write to can no longer shadow the standard library or an installed package. **(b) `misaki[en]>=0.9.4,<0.10` is a direct dependency**, although sci2audio does not import it: kokoro only asks for `misaki[en]>=0.9.4`, so a user's install could pick up a later misaki. D-121's offline guarantee relies on how misaki 0.9 loads its spaCy model (it downloads only when `is_package` is false). The deptry DEP002 ignore for misaki says why. **(c) The download stops a file as soon as it exceeds its listed size** (adds to D-122): `DownloadError`, nothing kept. A wrong or hostile response can no longer fill the disk before the sha256 check. The `download.py` docstring also notes that huggingface.co and github.com redirect to their download servers (Hugging Face CDN and LFS/Xet storage, objects.githubusercontent.com), and that the checksums make the content independent of the host; the consent screen should say so. Each change has a test that fails without it. Question: do the review findings change D-121 and D-122? Alternatives not chosen: (a) keep the folder at the front (it could shadow a module); (b) rely on kokoro's open bound (a later misaki could go online on first use); (c) rely on the checksum alone (it runs only after the whole body is written). Reason: smaller attack surface and the offline rule kept true for users' installs, not only for our lock. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:32 UTC, under D-80; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:32:04Z) | sci2audio Issue #18, PR #88 (review round 1, comment 6838897674; answer 6839049204); D-121, D-122 |
| D-140 | 2026-11-12 | The script job fails only when it cannot go on | The script job never stops to ask (D-61 (3)): a text step that fails is a problem in the report and its edits are left out, an `ask` edit reads the original and is listed, an unknown chapter index is ignored with a warning, a chosen chapter with nothing to read is listed as such. **It fails, with a `JobError` whose message a person can act on and with nothing half-written, only when it cannot go on**: a file type it can't read (refused before any work), a file it can't open, one that vanished or is damaged (extraction error), or a write that fails (a full disk). Question: which problems end a job, and which are only reported? Alternatives not chosen: stopping on a failing step (one faulty rule would block every file); carrying on after a write error (the files would not match the report). Reason: D-61's never-stop rule for everything the person can review afterwards; a clear stop where no useful result is possible. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:31 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:31:34Z) | sci2audio Issue #70, PR #90; plan `plans/issue70-script-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (12); D-61 |
| D-139 | 2026-11-12 | The settings file: platformdirs, JSON, chosen chapters not kept | The person's settings are kept in **`settings.json` in the user's configuration folder from `platformdirs.user_config_path("sci2audio", appauthor=False, roaming=True)`** (Windows `%APPDATA%\sci2audio`, macOS `~/Library/Application Support/sci2audio`, Linux `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/sci2audio`). New run-time dependency **platformdirs 4.14.5, MIT**, no dependencies. JSON, because the standard library reads and writes it (`tomllib` only reads), with `"version": 1`. It keeps every `JobSettings` field **except the chosen chapters**, which belong to one document. Only the command line and the server read and write it; a job gets its settings passed in and never touches the file (no global state). **A missing, unreadable or broken file never stops start-up**: what can't be read falls back to its default, with a warning the caller shows. Saving is atomic. Question: where do settings live, and in what format (playbook 3.4; a gap the v1.0 plan names in its app paragraph)? Alternatives not chosen: hand-written per-OS paths (about 20 lines, but platformdirs handles XDG, roaming profiles and macOS correctly and is widely used); TOML (needs a writer dependency). Reason: a small, MIT, widely used library for a detail that is easy to get wrong. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:31 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:31:34Z) | sci2audio Issue #70, PR #90; plan `plans/issue70-script-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (11); `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES.md` |
| D-138 | 2026-11-12 | One job at a time per JobRunner; progress as phases | **A `JobRunner` object runs one job at a time**: it owns a lock, and a second job on the same runner raises `JobBusyError`. The command line and the server each make one runner; there is no module-level state, so tests and a later web version can make their own. A job reports **progress as phases** (reading, layout, text steps, writing, done) through a callback, for the server's events (#76); the time estimate is #71's. Question: how is "one job at a time" kept without global state? Alternatives not chosen: a module-level lock (global state, against CLAUDE.md); a lock file in the working folder (only needed once jobs resume across processes, #71). Reason: the simplest rule that keeps jobs apart and stays testable. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:31 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:31:34Z); **extended by D-161** (13 Nov: the audio job also locks its working folder across processes) | sci2audio Issue #70, PR #90; plan `plans/issue70-script-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (10) |
| D-137 | 2026-11-12 | The job record `job.json` in the working folder | Each script job writes **`job.json`** (`"format": "sci2audio-job"`, `"version": 1`) to its working folder, **after** its outputs: sci2audio version, the source's name, format and SHA-256, the settings (without folders, which are machine-specific), the chapters with whether chosen and how many utterances, and the names of the files written. It is the shape the audio job (#71) builds on to resume: the same file and settings mean the same utterances, so finished chunks can be skipped. Question: what does the script job leave for resume, which belongs to #71? Alternatives not chosen: nothing (#71 would have to change the script job's output); writing the utterances themselves (the job is fast and deterministic, so they can be cut again). Reason: a small, versioned record that keeps #71's options open. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:31 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:31:34Z) | sci2audio Issue #70, PR #90; plan `plans/issue70-script-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (9) |
| D-136 | 2026-11-12 | Script job files and overwriting | The script job writes **`<name>.script.txt`, `<name>.report.json` and `<name>.report.md`** to the output folder (default: the source file's folder), `<name>` being the source's file name without its extension, made safe with output's file-name rules. **All three are written under partial names first and renamed only when all are complete**, so a full disk changes none of them and no half-written file is left. **Existing files are replaced only with `overwrite=True`**, checked before any work starts; a file that appears during the run is never replaced without it. The same rule as output (#69). Question: where do the files go, and may the job replace files that exist? Alternatives not chosen: silently replacing (could destroy a person's own file of that name); numbering new files (clutters the folder, breaks the link between script and report). Reason: never lose a person's file; one rule for every file the app writes. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:31 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:31:34Z); **refined by D-199** (13 Nov: the server's script job writes into the working folder's `script/` and always replaces it) | sci2audio Issue #70, PR #90; plan `plans/issue70-script-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (8); output #69 |
| D-135 | 2026-11-12 | The listening script: one utterance per line | The script file shows **exactly what the voice gets per call: one utterance per line**, a blank line between blocks, `[pause]` on its own line for a pause, and `# n. <title>` per chapter (numbered from 1); a chapter not chosen shows only its heading with "(not spoken)", a chosen chapter with nothing to read says "(nothing to read)". Question: what does the listening-script file look like? Alternatives not chosen: one paragraph per block (easier to read, but hides where sentences are cut, which matters for checking the cutter, D-115). Reason: what a person reads is what the voice will say, call by call. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:31 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:31:34Z) | sci2audio Issue #70, PR #90; plan `plans/issue70-script-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (7); D-115 |
| D-134 | 2026-11-12 | The report: versioned JSON and Markdown, owned by the app | The pre-analysis report (UC-01) is written as **JSON `"format": "sci2audio-report"`, `"version": 1`, owned by the app**, and as Markdown for reading. It holds **one entry per proposed edit**: category, step, kind, reason, status (`accepted`, `set-aside` with the step, kind and place of the edit that won, `switched-off`), `spoken` (false when the edit is not accepted or its chapter is not chosen), the original text as printed, what is said instead (the replacement; "" for a removal; `[pause]`; the original for `ask`), block and span, **the page as numbered and the page label as printed**, and its chapter. It also holds **counts per category** (applied, not spoken because the chapter is not chosen, set aside, switched off), the chapters (index, title, block range, first page, reference list, chosen, utterances), steps that failed, steps not run for the language, and warnings. Chapters count from 0 in the JSON (as in `JobSettings.chapters`) and from 1 for people. Question: what does the report hold, in what format (plan app paragraph, UC-01, Q15)? Alternatives not chosen: Markdown only (the interface and later tools need data); an unversioned JSON (readers could not tell changes apart). Reason: everything the person needs to check each change, referenceable by page as printed (Colin's design principle). | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:31 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:31:34Z) | sci2audio Issue #70, PR #90; plan `plans/issue70-script-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (6); D-105, D-128 |
| D-133 | 2026-11-12 | Chapters are chosen by index; unknown indices are ignored | `JobSettings.chapters` holds **the indices of the chosen chapters** in the job's chapter list, or **None for the default choice** (every chapter but the reference list). An index the document doesn't have is **ignored and named in the report's warnings**; it never stops the job. Question: how does a person's chapter choice reach the job? Alternatives not chosen: choosing by title (titles can repeat, e.g. two "Appendix" sections); raising an error for an unknown index (stops the job, against D-61 (3)). Reason: indices are unique and stable for one file, since layout is deterministic. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:31 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:31:34Z); **chapter order stays printed order in v1.0 by D-247** (13 Nov; reordering is sci2audio Issue #119) | sci2audio Issue #70, PR #90; plan `plans/issue70-script-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (5); D-100 |
| D-132 | 2026-11-12 | The reference list is found by its title, from one table | A chapter is the reference list when its title, **without its printed number and without case**, is one of the names in **one referenceable table in the code, `REFERENCE_NAMES`, by language**: English for v1.0 (References, Bibliography, Reference list, Works cited, Literature cited; D-50), German as an extra for the next language (Literatur, Literaturverzeichnis, Quellen, Quellenverzeichnis). Such a chapter is **deselected by default**; **appendices are never deselected** ("A Appendix", "Supplementary Material: Appendices"). Question: how does the app know which chapter is the reference list (D-100)? Alternatives not chosen: asking the reference-list text step (#64), which works on blocks, not chapters, and may be switched off. Reason: a heading name is how papers mark their reference list; one table keeps the names in one place. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:31 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:31:34Z) | sci2audio Issue #70, PR #90; plan `plans/issue70-script-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (4); D-100; TP-06 |
| D-131 | 2026-11-12 | The first chapter's title, and empty chapters | The chapter before the first heading, and the only chapter of a document without headings, is titled with **`Content.metadata.title`, else the file name without its extension** ("acl2023-hardt-ellipsis"), else "Untitled". **The front chapter exists whenever the first heading is not the first block**, even if it holds only furniture; a chapter with nothing to say is listed with 0 utterances, and the audio job (#71) gives it no audio. Question: what is the first chapter called when the file gives no title (the ACL and arXiv papers in the corpus give none), and what about a chapter with nothing to read? Alternatives not chosen: the file name with its extension (reads oddly as a chapter name); dropping chapters with nothing to read (the chapter list would change with the switches). Reason: a readable name, and a chapter list that depends on the document alone. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:31 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:31:34Z) | sci2audio Issue #70, PR #90; plan `plans/issue70-script-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (3); D-100 |
| D-130 | 2026-11-12 | Chapter boundaries: every block in exactly one chapter | **Each top-level section of the section tree starts a chapter, and a chapter runs to the next chapter's start or the document's end**, so every block is in exactly one chapter, also where the tree leaves gaps; blocks in a gap belong to the chapter before them. A top-level section of a deeper level (no level-1 heading above it) also starts a chapter. Titles are kept **as printed**, numbers included ("2 Related Work"), with runs of white space read as one space. Question: where exactly does a chapter (D-100) start and end? Alternatives not chosen: only level-1 sections (blocks under a stray top-level level-2 section would belong to no chapter); a chapter's own range only (blocks in gaps would be lost). Reason: nothing is lost, and the chapter list follows the printed headings. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:31 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:31:34Z) | sci2audio Issue #70, PR #90; plan `plans/issue70-script-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (2); D-100 |
| D-129 | 2026-11-12 | Text steps on the whole document; chapter choice applied when cutting | Implements D-100 in the script job (#70): **the text steps run on the whole document, whatever chapters are chosen**; **the chapter choice applies when the script is cut into utterances** (only the chosen chapters' script pieces are cut); **the report marks the edits in chapters not chosen as not spoken** (`spoken: false`, counted per category). A property test shows that cutting only the chosen chapters gives the same utterances as cutting the whole script and dropping the other chapters (the cutter never joins blocks, D-115). Question: how does the job combine text steps, chapter choice and report (plan Q6)? Alternatives not chosen: running the steps only on the chosen chapters (the report would be incomplete; every change of choice would need a new analysis). Reason: a complete report, and choosing chapters costs no second analysis. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:31 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:31:34Z) | sci2audio Issue #70, PR #90; plan `plans/issue70-script-job-plan.md` (project files) §9 (1); D-100, D-115 |
| D-128 | 2026-11-12 | A block step's accepted edit claims its whole block (refines D-105) | Refines D-105's "earlier step wins" for block steps (D-109). **Once a block step's edit is accepted for a block, it claims the whole block, its start and end included: every later edit in that block is set aside**, with that edit recorded as the one that won. Found in the review of sci2audio PR #83 (round 1, must-fix M1): by the core overlap rule (D-61 (2)) a mark at position 0 or at the end of a block touches no character, so it did not overlap a whole-block `remove`; an inline insertion ("Note: ") or a pause there was accepted, and the listener would hear it where a removed running head had been. Question: does a whole-block edit also block edits at its block's two ends? Alternatives not chosen: keep the core overlap rule only (whole-block removals could still leak an insertion or pause, against D-105's "whole-block decisions intact"); change the core overlap rule (a core change, and it would also forbid a pause after an ordinary inline edit's end, which is wanted). Reason: a block step decides about the whole block; the stricter rule sits in the runner, so the accepted edits still pass `core.check_edits`. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 23:13 UTC, under D-80; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:13:06Z) | sci2audio PR #83 (review round 1, M1; `plans/review-pr83-round1.md`, project files), Issue #63; D-61, D-105, D-109 |
| D-127 | 2026-11-12 | Intel Macs are not supported by the voice | PyTorch has published no macOS x86_64 wheels since 2.2, and sci2audio needs torch 2.6 or later for Kokoro (D-48). So **sci2audio supports Macs with Apple chips only**; the README says so, and the release install test (#80) covers macOS on Apple chips, Windows and Linux. Question: which Macs can run the voice? Alternatives not chosen: pinning an old torch for Intel Macs (no security fixes, a second set of tested versions); an ONNX runtime for Intel Macs (dropped by D-48). Reason: no supported torch exists for them. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 22:52 UTC, under D-80; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T22:52:31Z) | sci2audio Issue #18, PR #88; plan `plans/issue18-kokoro-plan.md` (project files); D-48; README |
| D-126 | 2026-11-12 | The CI licence check ignores espeakng-loader, which is checked by hand | `espeakng-loader` (pulled in by misaki, D-104) states **no licence in its package metadata**, so the `licences` CI job (`pip-licenses --fail-on UNKNOWN`) fails on it. Its repository's licence file says **MIT** for the loader, and the wheel bundles the espeak-ng library and data, **GPL-3.0-or-later**; both can be combined with GPL-3.0-or-later. The package is added to the job's `--ignore-packages` with a comment saying it was checked by hand, and both licences are recorded in `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES.md`. Question: how does CI treat a dependency whose metadata lacks a licence that was verified at the source? Alternatives not chosen: dropping the `UNKNOWN` check (it would let unknown licences through); replacing misaki's espeak-ng fallback (worse pronunciation of unknown words). Reason: the licence gate in `.claude/rules/licences.md` asks for the licence at the source, which was found, and every other package stays under the automatic check. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 22:52 UTC, under D-80; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T22:52:31Z) | sci2audio Issue #18, PR #88; plan `plans/issue18-kokoro-plan.md` (project files); `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES.md`; D-104 |
| D-125 | 2026-11-12 | Shape of the voice plugin contract: Abogen's engine and session, slimmed where its other files are not copied | The contract stays as D-52 copied it: `Engine.createSession()`, `EngineSession.synthesize(SynthesisRequest) -> SynthesizedAudio`, `dispose()` on both, Abogen's immutable value objects and error hierarchy, each copied file with Abogen's MIT notice and a list of the changes. **Changes**: a BCP 47 language tag replaces Abogen's `Language` enum; `SynthesizedAudio` carries its sample rate; `PCM_FLOAT32` (raw little-endian float32 mono) is the one format a voice returns, and `voices.wav` turns it into the 16-bit WAV files of D-117; `VoiceInfo` replaces `VoiceManifest`; `Engine.api_version` and `listVoices()` replace Abogen's manifest and `VoiceLister` capability, whose modules are not copied. No plugin discovery in v1.0 (PE-07 public plugins come later). The voice conformance suite lives in `sci2audio.testing.voices` (the layer of D-103, D-114) as plain functions without pytest. Question: how much of Abogen's plugin specification does sci2audio keep? Alternatives not chosen: a slimmer contract without sessions (the Abogen audit's suggestion; D-52 decided to copy the files); copying the whole plugin framework (about 1,350 lines, not needed before third-party voices). Reason: D-52's copy stays recognisable for later updates from Abogen, and the conformance suite fixes the contract for future voices. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 22:52 UTC, under D-80; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T23:15:37Z) | sci2audio Issue #18, PR #88; plan `plans/issue18-kokoro-plan.md` (project files); D-46, D-52, D-117; sci2audio Issue #6 |
| D-124 | 2026-11-12 | Python range stays 3.12 or later; pipx users need Python 3.12 for now | kokoro 0.9.4 and misaki 0.9.4 declare `Requires-Python <3.13`. uv ignores upper bounds, so `uv tool install sci2audio` works on Python 3.13 (tested in #18: the voice speaks offline on 3.13), but pip and pipx refuse to install on 3.13 or later. Decision: **keep `requires-python = ">=3.12"`**, tell pipx users in the README to use Python 3.12 (`pipx install --python python3.12 sci2audio`), and track the upstream cap in **sci2audio Issue #87** (v1.0 milestone, checked before the release in #80). Question: does sci2audio copy its dependencies' Python cap? Alternative not chosen: capping sci2audio at `<3.13` (blocks 3.13 for every user because of a metadata cap that is not a real break). Reason: uv, the recommended installer (D-47), is unaffected, and the cap can be dropped when kokoro and misaki release. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 22:52 UTC, under D-80; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T22:52:31Z) | sci2audio Issues #18, #87, #80, PR #88; README; D-47 |
| D-123 | 2026-11-12 | Linux install size: CPU-only PyTorch in our own lock and CI, and a documented Linux command for users (VA-02) | Measured on 12 Nov 2026: with torch from PyPI, the Linux environment is **5.6 GB** (3.0 GB NVIDIA libraries, 1.0 GB triton, 1.1 GB torch); with the PyTorch CPU index it is **1.3 GB**. Published package metadata cannot name an index. Decision: **our own lock takes torch from the PyTorch CPU index on Linux** (`[tool.uv.sources]` in `pyproject.toml`), so CI and contributors install 1.3 GB (D-48: CI uses CPU-only PyTorch); **the README gives Linux users `uv tool install sci2audio --torch-backend cpu`**, and the plain command keeps working (it installs the GPU build, which uses an NVIDIA GPU when there is one). Question: does sci2audio document a CPU index for Linux or accept the CUDA-sized install (plan review, VA-02)? Alternative not chosen: accepting the 5.6 GB install as the only path. Reason: one documented flag cuts the Linux install by more than 4 GB without breaking the one-command install (WI-01). | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 22:52 UTC, under D-80; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T22:52:31Z) | sci2audio Issue #18, PR #88; plan `plans/issue18-kokoro-plan.md` (project files); D-47, D-48; `docs/baseline.md`; README |
| D-122 | 2026-11-12 | The voice download: one function, two pinned sources, safe to interrupt; HF_HUB_OFFLINE=1 at all times (refines D-104) | `voices.download_plan()` lists every file of the download with URL, size, sha256 and licence, for the consent screen (VA-03); `voices.download_voice_files(models_dir, progress=...)` fetches what is missing: the Kokoro config and weights and **all 28 English voice packs** from `hexgrad/Kokoro-82M` at revision `f3ff3571791e39611d31c381e3a41a3af07b4987`, and the `en_core_web_sm` 3.8.0 wheel from explosion's GitHub release, about **355 MB** in total (D-47's "everything for offline use"). The command line (#72) and the server (#76, #77) call these two and nothing else. Each file is streamed to `.part`, checked against its pinned sha256, then renamed; a rerun skips correct files, so an interrupted download resumes. **One HTTP client, `requests`, for both sources; `huggingface_hub` never downloads anything.** So **`HF_HUB_OFFLINE=1` is set at all times**, not "except during the user-started download" as D-104 said. That is stricter, with the same intent. The engine loads the model and voices only from local files and raises `ModelNotFoundError` when one is missing, never downloading. The caller passes the models folder (no global state); the app picks its default (#70, #71). Question: how is D-104's one download function built, and where may the network be used? Alternatives not chosen: `huggingface_hub.hf_hub_download` for the model plus a second client for the wheel (two code paths, and huggingface_hub would need to be online during the download); downloading single voices on demand (each voice is 0.5 MB, and partial states complicate offline use). Reason: one tested path, integrity by checksum, and no library that could go online on its own. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 22:52 UTC, under D-80; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T22:52:31Z); **refined by D-141** (12 Nov: the download stops a file as soon as it exceeds its listed size) | sci2audio Issue #18, PR #88; plan `plans/issue18-kokoro-plan.md` (project files); D-47, D-104; sci2audio Issues #72, #76, #77, #71 |
| D-121 | 2026-11-12 | The spaCy English model is unpacked into the model folder and put on sys.path (D-104's choice) | D-104 left a choice to the #18 plan: put the `en_core_web_sm` wheel on `sys.path` or install it into the environment. Decision: **the download unpacks the wheel into `<models folder>/spacy/en_core_web_sm-3.8.0/`** (into a temporary folder that is renamed when complete), and `voices.prepare_environment()` puts that folder at the front of `sys.path`. Then `spacy.util.is_package` finds the model and misaki never calls `spacy.cli.download`. As a second guard, the engine checks for the model itself and raises `ModelNotFoundError` instead of letting misaki download it. Tested on 12 Nov 2026: Kokoro speaks with every network socket blocked on Python 3.12 and 3.13; a fast test patches `spacy.cli.download` to fail. Question: how does the model reach spaCy without a network call at run time? | D-120 | 2026-11-12 | Licence facts for the FFmpeg builds bundled by imageio-ffmpeg 0.8.1, and how output runs them (Q5, D-99) | Records what the #69 thread found on 12 Nov 2026 by downloading all seven imageio-ffmpeg 0.8.1 wheels from PyPI and reading each binary's build configuration and encoder list. **Linux x86_64 and aarch64 (n9.0.2), macOS x86_64 (9.0.2), Windows x86_64 (9.0.2), Windows arm64 (9.0.1) and Windows 32-bit (4.2.2, an old build)** are built with `--enable-gpl --enable-version3`, so **GPL-3.0-or-later**. **macOS arm64 (9.0)** is built with `--enable-gpl` only, so **GPL-2.0-or-later**. None is built with `--enable-nonfree`. Every build includes libmp3lame (LAME, LGPL-2.0-or-later), FFmpeg's native aac encoder, and the `ipod` (M4B) and `ffmetadata` formats. No wheel ships ffprobe. The wrapper is BSD-2-Clause, and `get_ffmpeg_exe()` makes no network call. sci2audio does not redistribute the binaries, because they arrive from PyPI as a dependency. A future native installer that bundles them must also offer the FFmpeg source. **Output opens every ffmpeg input with `-protocol_whitelist file,pipe`**, so ffmpeg cannot open a network address even if a path looks like one (CLAUDE.md: no network call the user did not start). Tests check that every `-i` carries it and that ffmpeg refuses an `http://` input. Question: may sci2audio depend on these builds, and under which licences? Alternatives not chosen: PyAV (in-process, a larger surface, D-99); requiring a system ffmpeg (breaks the one-command install, WI-01). Reason: both GPL licences can be combined with GPL-3.0-or-later; nothing nonfree is included; MP3 and AAC encoding work on every platform. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 22:47 UTC, under D-80; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T22:47:40Z) | sci2audio Issue #69, PR #85; `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES.md` (section "Bundled programs"); D-99 (Q5); plan `plans/issue69-output-plan.md` (project files) |
| D-119 | 2026-11-12 | Output never overwrites files unless asked; partial files and the read-along file as a completeness mark (#69 point D) | The output stage writes each file as `.<name>.partial` in the target folder and renames it when complete. Any error, Ctrl-C included, removes the partial file. A later run clears partial files left by a process that was killed outright. **Existing output files are replaced only with `overwrite=True`**; otherwise the run stops with `FileExistsError` before writing anything. The app's resume (#71) passes `overwrite=True` for its own job folder. **The read-along file is written last**, so its presence means every audio file it names is complete. An overwrite run removes the old read-along file first, so it never vouches for a half-redone set. Completed files of a failed run stay in place for a rerun. Question: what may output do with files already in the output folder? Alternatives not chosen: always overwriting (deleting or overwriting user files is risk:high, CLAUDE.md); writing into a staging folder and moving everything at the end (a vanished or full target folder still fails halfway, and it needs twice the disk space). Reason: no half-written file is ever left (#10), and nothing the user made is replaced unless the app asks. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 22:47 UTC, under D-80; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T22:47:40Z) | sci2audio Issue #69, PR #85, Issue #10, Issue #71; plan `plans/issue69-output-plan.md` (project files); D-102 |

| D-117 | 2026-11-12 | Chunk audio is 16-bit mono PCM WAV, one sample rate per job; timing comes from the frame counts (#69 point B) | **A contract between voices/app and output.** The audio file of every `AudioChunk` is a **WAV file with 16-bit PCM, one channel, and one sample rate for the whole job**; the voice (#18) or the audio job (#71) writes it. Output reads the headers with the standard library (`wave`) and refuses any other format, a second rate, or a missing file, naming the chunk, before it writes anything. **All times come from the WAV frame counts**, not from `AudioChunk.duration`, which the voice may have rounded. Output streams the samples into ffmpeg's stdin, once per chapter for the MP3s and once for the book for the M4B, with silence as zero samples. So no temporary WAV is written, and chapter marks and read-along times are exact to the sample. No core change: `AudioChunk` stays as it is. Question: in what format do chunks reach output, and where does timing come from? Alternative not chosen: any format ffmpeg reads, with each chunk decoded by its own ffmpeg process (about ten minutes extra on a 10,000-sentence book, and timing only as exact as the decoder). Reason: exact read-along timing (OU-02) at no extra cost; Kokoro produces mono audio at one rate anyway. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 22:47 UTC, under D-80; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T22:47:40Z) | sci2audio Issues #69, #18, #71, PR #85; plan `plans/issue69-output-plan.md` (project files); D-61, D-102 |
| D-116 | 2026-11-12 | Pauses between sentences and at clause cuts; where the chapter pause goes (#69 point A, extends Q8) | Adds to the Q8 pause defaults of the remaining-work plan (2.0 s before a chapter, 1.0 s after a heading, 0.6 s between paragraphs, 0.5 s for a `pause` edit), which have no entry of their own and were set in sci2audio Issue #69. **A fifth output setting, `sentence_pause`, defaults to 0.3 s** between two sentences of one block. **A clause cut inside a long sentence** (the Kokoro length fallback of Q4, D-98) gets **no pause** (`Pause.NONE`). The app gives every chunk one pause kind (none, sentence, pause edit, paragraph, heading; `Pause.strongest` combines two reasons). Output turns the kinds into seconds. **The chapter pause opens every chapter, the first included**, whatever kind its first chunk carries. So every chapter file begins the same way, and a jump to a chapter mark lands just before the speech. Question: how long is the pause between sentences, which Q8 did not set, and where does the chapter pause go? Alternatives not chosen: 0 s between sentences (Kokoro clips have almost no trailing silence, so sentences run together); putting the chapter pause at the end of the previous chapter (the first chapter would start without it, and every MP3 would end in silence). Reason: speech that sounds natural, and the same structure in every chapter. The value is a setting, so a listening test can tune it. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 22:47 UTC, under D-80; Colin informed in the project chat at 2026-11-12T22:47:40Z) | sci2audio Issue #69, PR #85; plan `plans/issue69-output-plan.md` (project files); Q8 of `plans/v1-remaining-plan.md`, D-98, D-61 |
| D-115 | 2026-11-12 | Utterance cutter: where its type lives, the length limit and the fallback cut (refines D-98) | Details of the utterance cutter D-98 gave to textprep, for PR B of sci2audio Issue #63. (1) The cutter returns textprep's own **`Utterance`** (text, span, pause flag), not a core type: the voice gets only the text, and the app makes one `AudioChunk` from each utterance's span; no core change. (2) A sentence ends after `.`, `?` or `!` (closing quotes or brackets allowed) followed by white space and an upper-case letter, a digit or an opening quote or bracket, except after an English abbreviation (Fig., Eq., et al., e.g., i.e., vs., Dr., approx., …) or a single initial; other languages use the punctuation rule without the abbreviation list until they get one. (3) **`max_chars` defaults to 300** and is a parameter, so the voice issue (#18) can set Kokoro's real per-call limit (about 510 phoneme tokens). (4) **Fallback order** for a longer sentence: the last `;` or `:` before the limit, else the last comma, else a dash, else the last space; a single token over the limit is cut hard. (5) Utterance spans never overlap: a cut is not placed inside a replacement unless the replacement alone is over the limit, and then its later parts get an empty span at the replacement's end. Question: what does the cutter return, and how does it cut a sentence that is too long? Alternatives not chosen: an `Utterance` type in core (a core change for something only textprep makes and only the app reads); a limit fixed in code (the voice knows its own limit); cutting at any space first (breaks clauses where a clause mark was available). Reason: keeps core unchanged, keeps read-along spans exact, and lets #18 tune the limit. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 22:45 UTC, under D-80; plan approved as written; Colin informed in the project chat at 22:45:11 UTC); **refined by D-157** (13 Nov: `Utterance.continues` marks the parts after a length cut) | sci2audio Issue #63, PR #83; plan `plans/issue63-text-steps-plan.md` (project files) §5 |

| D-105 | 2026-11-12 | Which edit wins when two text steps propose overlapping edits | Settles what D-61 (2) left to the app. The built-in text steps sit in one **fixed registry ordered coarse to fine**: steps that remove or replace a whole block (page furniture, the reference list, placeholder notices, footnote handling) come before steps that work inside a block. When two proposed edits overlap, **the edit of the earlier step wins**; the other is **set aside** and listed in the pre-analysis report with its reason. An `ask` edit reads the original until the user answers (D-61 (3)); **no built-in v1.0 step may propose `ask`**, because nothing in v1.0 answers it (the editable script, UC-04, is a Should), but the runner and the report support it. Question: how are overlapping edits settled (plan Q15)? Alternatives not chosen: a priority number per edit (more to get wrong, no gain for built-in steps); any order with "earlier wins" (a URL edit inside a reference block would set aside the removal of the whole block). Reason: one written, testable rule; coarse-to-fine keeps whole-block decisions intact. | **Decided (Claude)** (coordinator, 12 Nov 22:39 UTC, under D-80; Colin informed in the project chat at 22:39 UTC); **refined by D-109** (12 Nov: stages `block` and `inline`; the registry check refuses a block step after an inline step) **and D-128** (12 Nov: a block step's accepted edit claims its whole block, start and end included; later edits in it are set aside); **refined by D-150** (12 Nov: the two steps of Characters and hyphenation run last among the inline steps) | sci2audio Issues #63, #70; plan `plans/v1-remaining-plan.md` (project files) §4; D-57, D-61 |
